class="interlanguage-link-target"><span>ئۇيغۇرچە / Uyghurche</span></a></li><li class="interlanguage-link interwiki-vi mw-list-item"><a href="https://vi.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%E1%BB%95ng_l%C3%A3nh_thi%C3%AAn_s%E1%BB%A9_Gabriel" title="Tổng lãnh thiên sứ Gabriel – Vietnamese" lang="vi" hreflang="vi" class="interlanguage-link-target"><span>Tiếng Việt</span></a></li><li class="interlanguage-link interwiki-wa mw-list-item"><a href="https://wa.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andje_Gabriyel" title="Andje Gabriyel 					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Angel in Abrahamic religions</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Gabriel_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Gabriel (disambiguation)">Gabriel (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable"><span>"Angel Gabriel" redirects here. For the American comic book artist, see <a href="/wiki/Angel_Gabriele" title="Angel Gabriele">Angel Gabriele</a>.</span> <span>"Jibrail" redirects here. For the village in Iran, see <a href="/wiki/Jebreil" title="Jebreil">Jebreil</a>.</span></div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066479718">.mw-parser-output .infobox-subbox{padding:0;border:none;margin:-3px;width:auto;min-width:100%;font-size:100%;clear:none;float:none;background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .infobox-3cols-child{margin:auto}.mw-parser-output .infobox .navbar{font-size:100%}body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-header,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-subheader,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-above,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-title,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-image,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-full-data,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-below{text-align:center}</style><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div class="honorific-prefix" style="display:inline;font-size: 77%; font-weight: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">Saint</a></div><br /><div style="display:inline;" class="fn">Gabriel</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><div class="">
<div style="width: 270px; height: 300px; overflow: hidden;">
<div style="position: relative; top: -px; left: -50px; width: 768px"><div class="noresize"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Leonardo_Da_Vinci_-_Annunciazione.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Leonardo_Da_Vinci_-_Annunciazione.jpeg/768px-Leonardo_Da_Vinci_-_Annunciazione.jpeg" decoding="async" width="768" height="351" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Leonardo_Da_Vinci_-_Annunciazione.jpeg/1152px-Leonardo_Da_Vinci_-_Annunciazione.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Leonardo_Da_Vinci_-_Annunciazione.jpeg/1536px-Leonardo_Da_Vinci_-_Annunciazione.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="4062" data-file-height="1856" /></a></span></div></div>
</div>
</div><div class="infobox-caption">Detail of Gabriel from the <i><a href="/wiki/Annunciation_(Leonardo_da_Vinci)" class="mw-redirect" title="Annunciation (Leonardo da Vinci)">Annunciation</a> by <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci" title="Leonardo da Vinci">Leonardo da Vinci</a> (1470s)</i></div></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;">Archangel, Angel of Revelation, Commander of the Powers</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Samaritanism" title="Samaritanism">Samaritanism</a> <br />All Christian denominations which <a href="/wiki/Veneration_of_saints" class="mw-redirect" title="Veneration of saints">venerate saints</a><br />Judaism<br /> Islam<br />and others<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;N 1&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1126788409">.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul{line-height:inherit;list-style:none;margin:0;padding:0}.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol li,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul li{margin-bottom:0}</style><div class="plainlist">
<ul><li><b>29 September</b> with angels <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Michael</a> and <a href="/wiki/Raphael_(archangel)" title="Raphael (archangel)">Raphael</a> (Catholic Church) (post-1969)</li>
<li><b>24 March</b> (<a href="/wiki/Western_Rite_Orthodoxy" title="Western Rite Orthodoxy">Western Rite Orthodoxy</a> and General Roman Calendar before 1969)</li>
<li><b>26 March</b>, <b>13 July</b> (Eastern Orthodox Church)</li>
<li><b>13 <a href="/wiki/Paoni" title="Paoni">Paoni</a></b>, <b>22 <a href="/wiki/Koiak" title="Koiak">Koiak</a></b> and <b>26 <a href="/wiki/Paoni" title="Paoni">Paoni</a></b> (Coptic Church)</li></ul>
</div>
<ul><li>28 of December (Tahsas 19) and 26 of July (Hamle 19) Ethiopian Calendar</li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Clothed in blue or white garments;<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Carrying a lily,<sup id="cite_ref-Ronner1993_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ronner1993-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> a trumpet,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> a shining lantern,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> a branch from Paradise,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> a scroll,<sup id="cite_ref-Ronner1993_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ronner1993-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and a scepter.<sup id="cite_ref-Ronner1993_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ronner1993-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Telecommunication workers,<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Guiley2004p140_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guiley2004p140-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> radio broadcasters,<sup id="cite_ref-Guiley2004p140_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guiley2004p140-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> messengers,<sup id="cite_ref-Guiley2004p140_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guiley2004p140-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> postal workers,<sup id="cite_ref-Guiley2004p140_4-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guiley2004p140-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> clerics,<sup id="cite_ref-Guiley2004p140_4-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guiley2004p140-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> diplomats,<sup id="cite_ref-Guiley2004p140_4-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guiley2004p140-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> stamp collectors,<sup id="cite_ref-Guiley2004p140_4-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guiley2004p140-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Portugal" title="Portugal">Portugal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Santander,_Cebu" title="Santander, Cebu">Santander</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cebu" title="Cebu">Cebu</a>, ambassadors</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Abrahamic_religions" title="Abrahamic religions">Abrahamic religions</a> (<a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Judaism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a> and <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>), <b>Gabriel</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span><span title="/eɪ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;face&#39;">eɪ</span><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">GAY</span>-bree-əl</i></a>)<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;N 2&#93;</a></sup> is an <a href="/wiki/Archangel" title="Archangel">archangel</a> with the power to announce God's will to humans. He is mentioned in the <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_Bible" title="Hebrew Bible">Hebrew Bible</a>, the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a>. Many Christian traditions&#160;– including <a href="/wiki/Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicism">Catholicism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy" title="Eastern Orthodoxy">Eastern Orthodoxy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheranism</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglicanism</a>&#160;– revere Gabriel as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Zimmerman_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zimmerman-6">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Blersch2019_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Blersch2019-7">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the Hebrew Bible, Gabriel appears to the prophet <a href="/wiki/Daniel_(biblical_figure)" title="Daniel (biblical figure)">Daniel</a> to explain his visions (<a href="/wiki/Daniel_8" title="Daniel 8">Daniel 8</a>:15–26, <a href="/wiki/Daniel_9" class="mw-redirect" title="Daniel 9">9</a>:21–27). The archangel also appears in the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Enoch" title="Book of Enoch">Book of Enoch</a> and other ancient Jewish writings not preserved in Hebrew. Alongside the archangel <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Michael</a>, Gabriel is described as the <a href="/wiki/Guardian_angel" title="Guardian angel">guardian angel</a> of <a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israel</a>, defending its people against the angels of the other nations. 
</p><p>In the New Testament, the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a> relates the stories of the <a href="/wiki/Annunciation" title="Annunciation">Annunciation</a>, in which the angel Gabriel appears to <a href="/wiki/Zechariah_(New_Testament_figure)" title="Zechariah (New Testament figure)">Zechariah</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Mary_(mother_of_Jesus)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary (mother of Jesus)">Virgin Mary</a>, announcing to each the births of <a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist" title="John the Baptist">John the Baptist</a> / <a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist_in_Islam" title="John the Baptist in Islam">Yahya</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> / <a href="/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam" title="Jesus in Islam">Isa</a>, respectively (<a href="#New_Testament">Luke 1:11–38</a>). 
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a> regards Gabriel as an archangel sent by God to various prophets, including <a href="/wiki/Muhammad_in_Islam" title="Muhammad in Islam">Muhammad</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-EoQ_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EoQ-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> The first five verses of the <i><a href="/wiki/Al-Alaq" title="Al-Alaq">Al-Alaq</a></i>, the 96th chapter of the Quran, is believed by Muslims to have been the first verses revealed by Gabriel to Muhammad.<sup id="cite_ref-EoQ_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EoQ-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Etymology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The name Gabriel (<a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew:</a> גַּבְרִיאֵל, <i>Gaḇrīʾēl</i>) is composed of the first person singular possessive form of the Hebrew noun <i>gever</i> (גֶּבֶר), meaning "man", and <i>ʾĒl,</i> meaning "God". This would make the translation of the <a href="/wiki/Archangel" title="Archangel">archangel's</a> name "God is my man".<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Arabic" title="Arabic">Arabic</a> he is called Jibrīl (جبريل).
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Gabriel_and_Vohu_Manah">Gabriel and Vohu Manah</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Gabriel and Vohu Manah">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The Zoroastrian "holy immortal" (<a href="/wiki/Amesha_Spenta" title="Amesha Spenta">Amesha Spentas</a>) Vohu Manah (or "good mind"), played a similar role to Gabriel in Zoroastrianism, appearing to <a href="/wiki/Zoroaster" title="Zoroaster">Zarathustra</a> and revealing the true nature of God and his covenant with man.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Judaism">Judaism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Judaism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Hebrew_Bible">Hebrew Bible</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Hebrew Bible">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the Hebrew Bible, Gabriel appears to the prophet <a href="/wiki/Daniel_(biblical_figure)" title="Daniel (biblical figure)">Daniel</a> to explain his visions (<a href="/wiki/Daniel_8" title="Daniel 8">Daniel 8</a>:15–26, <a href="/wiki/Daniel_9" class="mw-redirect" title="Daniel 9">9</a>:21–27). Later the angel Michael also appears to him (<a href="/wiki/Daniel_10" class="mw-redirect" title="Daniel 10">Daniel 10</a>:13, 21, <a href="/wiki/Daniel_12" class="mw-redirect" title="Daniel 12">Daniel 12</a>,1). These are the first instances of a named angel in the Bible. Gabriel's main function in Daniel is that of revealer,  responsible for interpreting Daniel's visions, a role he continues to have in later traditions.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Rabbinic_Judaism">Rabbinic Judaism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Rabbinic Judaism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Gabriel, (<a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>: <span lang="he" dir="rtl">גַּבְרִיאֵל</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Hebrew" title="Romanization of Hebrew">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Hebrew-language romanization"><i lang="he-Latn">Gaḇrīʾēl</i></span>) is interpreted by <a href="/wiki/Talmud" title="Talmud">Talmudic</a> <a href="/wiki/Rabbi" title="Rabbi">rabbis</a> to be the "man in linen" mentioned in the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Daniel" title="Book of Daniel">Book of Daniel</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Ezekiel" title="Book of Ezekiel">Book of Ezekiel</a>. Talmudic Judaism understands the angel in the Book of Ezekiel, who was sent to destroy <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a>, to be Gabriel. According to the <i><a href="/wiki/The_Jewish_Encyclopedia" title="The Jewish Encyclopedia">Jewish Encyclopedia</a></i>, Gabriel takes the form of a man, and stands at the left hand of God.<sup id="cite_ref-je_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-je-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Shimon_ben_Lakish" title="Shimon ben Lakish">Shimon ben Lakish</a> (<a href="/wiki/Syria_Palaestina" title="Syria Palaestina">Syria Palaestina</a>, 3rd century) concluded that the angelic names of Michael, Raphael, and Gabriel came out of the Babylonian exile (Gen. Rab. 48:9).<sup id="cite_ref-everson_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-everson-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Alongside the archangel <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Michael</a>, Gabriel is described as the <a href="/wiki/Patron" class="mw-redirect" title="Patron">guardian angel</a> of Israel, defending this people against the angels of the other nations.<sup id="cite_ref-philologos.org_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-philologos.org-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mystical_Judaism">Mystical Judaism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Mystical Judaism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Kabbalah" title="Kabbalah">Kabbalistic tradition</a>, Gabriel is identified with the <a href="/wiki/Sephirah" class="mw-redirect" title="Sephirah">sephirah</a> of <a href="/wiki/Yesod" title="Yesod">Yesod</a>. Gabriel also has a prominent role as one of God's archangels in the Kabbalah literature. There, Gabriel is portrayed as working in concert with Michael as part of God's court. Gabriel is not to be prayed to because only God can answer prayers and sends Gabriel as his agent.<sup id="cite_ref-je_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-je-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Jewish_mythology" title="Jewish mythology">Jewish mythology</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Garden_of_Eden" title="Garden of Eden">Garden of Eden</a> there is a <a href="/wiki/Tree_of_life_(biblical)" title="Tree of life (biblical)">tree of life</a> or the "tree of souls"<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> that blossoms and produces new <a href="/wiki/Souls" class="mw-redirect" title="Souls">souls</a>, which fall into the <a href="/wiki/Guf" title="Guf">Guf</a>, the <i>Treasury of Souls</i>. Gabriel reaches into the treasury and takes out the first soul that comes into his hand. Then <a href="/wiki/Lailah" class="mw-redirect" title="Lailah">Lailah</a>, the Angel of Conception, watches over the embryo until it is born.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Christianity">Christianity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Christianity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="New_Testament">New Testament</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: New Testament">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Gabriel's first appearance in the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>, concerns the annunciation of the birth of <a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist" title="John the Baptist">John the Baptist</a>. John's father <a href="/wiki/Zechariah_(priest)" class="mw-redirect" title="Zechariah (priest)">Zacharias</a>, a priest of the course of Abia, (<a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#1:5" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 1:5–7</a>) was childless because his wife <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_(biblical_figure)" title="Elizabeth (biblical figure)">Elisabeth</a> was barren. An angel appears to Zacharias while he is ministering in the Temple, to announce the birth of his son. When Zacharias questions the angel, the angel gives his name as Gabriel:
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ANGELICO,_Fra_Annunciation,_1437-46_(2236990916).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/ANGELICO%2C_Fra_Annunciation%2C_1437-46_%282236990916%29.jpg/220px-ANGELICO%2C_Fra_Annunciation%2C_1437-46_%282236990916%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="153" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/ANGELICO%2C_Fra_Annunciation%2C_1437-46_%282236990916%29.jpg/330px-ANGELICO%2C_Fra_Annunciation%2C_1437-46_%282236990916%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/ANGELICO%2C_Fra_Annunciation%2C_1437-46_%282236990916%29.jpg/440px-ANGELICO%2C_Fra_Annunciation%2C_1437-46_%282236990916%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1008" data-file-height="700" /></a><figcaption>Gabriel announcing the incarnation to Mary, <a href="/wiki/Annunciation_(Fra_Angelico,_San_Marco)" title="Annunciation (Fra Angelico, San Marco)">by Fra Angelico</a>, c. 1440–1445 (Convent of San Marco, Florence)</figcaption></figure>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><div class="poem">
<p>10 And the whole multitude of the people were praying without at the time of incense.<br />
11 And there appeared unto him an angel of the Lord standing on the right side of the altar of incense.<br />
12 And when Zacharias saw <i>him</i>, he was troubled, and fear fell upon him.<br />
13 But the angel said unto him, Fear not, Zacharias: for thy prayer is heard; and thy wife Elisabeth shall bear thee a son, and thou shalt call his name John.<br />
14 And thou shalt have joy and gladness; and many shall rejoice at his birth.<br />
15 For he shall be great in the sight of the Lord, and shall drink neither wine nor strong drink; and he shall be filled with the Holy Ghost, even from his mother's womb.<br />
16 And many of the children of Israel shall he turn to the Lord their God.<br />
17 And he shall go before him in the spirit and power of Elias, to turn the hearts of the fathers to the children, and the disobedient to the wisdom of the just; to make ready a people prepared for the Lord.<br />
18 And Zacharias said unto the angel, Whereby shall I know this? for I am an old man, and my wife well stricken in years.<br />
19 And the angel answering said unto him, I am Gabriel, that stand in the presence of God; and am sent to speak unto thee, and to shew thee these glad tidings.<br />
20 And, behold, thou shalt be dumb, and not able to speak, until the day that these things shall be performed, because thou believest not my words, which shall be fulfilled in their season.
</p>
</div><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite>Luke 1:10-20<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>After completing his required week<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> of ministry, Zacharias returns to his home and his wife Elizabeth conceives. After she has completed five months of her pregnancy (<a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#1:21" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 1:21–25</a>), Gabriel appears again, now to Mary, to announce the birth of Jesus:
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Annunciation,_Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art,_W1899-1-1-pma,_by_Henry_Ossawa_Tanner.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/The_Annunciation%2C_Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%2C_W1899-1-1-pma%2C_by_Henry_Ossawa_Tanner.jpg/220px-The_Annunciation%2C_Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%2C_W1899-1-1-pma%2C_by_Henry_Ossawa_Tanner.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/The_Annunciation%2C_Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%2C_W1899-1-1-pma%2C_by_Henry_Ossawa_Tanner.jpg/330px-The_Annunciation%2C_Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%2C_W1899-1-1-pma%2C_by_Henry_Ossawa_Tanner.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/The_Annunciation%2C_Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%2C_W1899-1-1-pma%2C_by_Henry_Ossawa_Tanner.jpg/440px-The_Annunciation%2C_Philadelphia_Museum_of_Art%2C_W1899-1-1-pma%2C_by_Henry_Ossawa_Tanner.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4096" data-file-height="3286" /></a><figcaption><i>The Annunciation</i>, by <a href="/wiki/Henry_Ossawa_Tanner" title="Henry Ossawa Tanner">Henry Ossawa Tanner</a> (1898)</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><div class="poem">
<p>26 And in the sixth month the angel Gabriel was sent from God unto a city of Galilee, named Nazareth,<br />
27 To a virgin espoused to a man whose name was Joseph, of the house of David; and the virgin's name <i>was</i> Mary.<br />
28 And the angel came in unto her, and said, Hail, <i>thou that art</i> highly favoured, the Lord <i>is</i> with thee: blessed <i>art</i> thou among women.<br />
29 And when she saw <i>him</i>, she was troubled at his saying, and cast in her mind what manner of salutation this should be.<br />
30 And the angel said unto her, Fear not, Mary: for thou hast found favour with God.<br />
31 And, behold, thou shalt conceive in thy womb, and bring forth a son, and shalt call his name JESUS.<br />
32 He shall be great, and shall be called the Son of the Highest: and the Lord God shall give unto him the throne of his father David:<br />
33 And he shall reign over the house of Jacob for ever; and of his kingdom there shall be no end.<br />
34 Then said Mary unto the angel, How shall this be, seeing I know not a man?<br />
35 And the angel answered and said unto her, The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee, and the power of the Highest shall overshadow thee: therefore also that holy thing which shall be born of thee shall be called the Son of God.<br />
36 And, behold, thy cousin Elisabeth, she hath also conceived a son in her old age: and this is the sixth month with her, who was called barren.<br />
37 For with God nothing shall be impossible.<br />
38 And Mary said, Behold the handmaid of the Lord; be it unto me according to thy word. And the angel departed from her.
</p>
</div><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite>Luke 1:26-38<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>Gabriel only appears by name in those two passages in Luke. In the first passage the angel identified himself as Gabriel, but in the second it is Luke who identified him as Gabriel. The only other named angels in the New Testament are Michael the Archangel (in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Jude#1:9" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Jude">Jude 1:9</a>) and <a href="/wiki/Abaddon" title="Abaddon">Abaddon</a> (in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Revelation#9:11" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Revelation">Revelation 9:11</a>). Believers are expressly warned not to worship angels in two New Testament passages: <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Colossians#2:18" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Colossians">Colossians 2:18–19</a> and <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Revelation#19:10" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Revelation">Revelation 19:10</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Writing_better_articles#Stay_on_topic" title="Wikipedia:Writing better articles"><span title="The material near this tag may contain information that is not relevant to the article&#39;s main topic. (January 2023)">relevant?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Intertestamental_literature">Intertestamental literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Intertestamental literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Gabriel is not called an archangel in the canonical Bible. However, the <a href="/wiki/Intertestamental_period" title="Intertestamental period">intertestamental period</a> (roughly 200 BC – 50 AD) produced a wealth of <a href="/wiki/Non-canonical_books_referenced_in_the_Bible" title="Non-canonical books referenced in the Bible">literature</a>, much of it having an <a href="/wiki/Apocalypse" title="Apocalypse">apocalyptic</a> orientation. The names and ranks of angels and devils were greatly expanded in this literature, and each had particular duties and status before God. This was the period when Gabriel was first referred to as an archangel.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Enoch" title="Book of Enoch">1 Enoch 9</a>:1–3, Gabriel, along with <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Michael</a>, <a href="/wiki/Uriel_(angel)" class="mw-redirect" title="Uriel (angel)">Uriel</a> and <a href="/wiki/Suriel" class="mw-redirect" title="Suriel">Suriel</a>, "saw much blood being shed upon the earth" (9:1) and heard the souls of men cry, "Bring our cause before the Most High" (<a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Book_of_Enoch/Chapter_09" class="extiw" title="s:Book of Enoch/Chapter 09">9:3</a>). In 1 Enoch 10:1, the reply came from "the Most High, the Holy and Great One" who sent forth agents, including Gabriel—
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>And the Lord said to Gabriel: "'Proceed against the bastards and the reprobates, and against the children of fornication: and destroy [the children of fornication and] the children of the Watchers from amongst men [and cause them to go forth]: send them one against the other that they may destroy each other in battle: for length of days shall they not have."</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Book%20of%20Enoch/Chapter%2010">1 Enoch 10:9</a></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>Gabriel is the fifth of the five angels who keep watch: "Gabriel, one of the holy angels, who is over Paradise and the serpents and the Cherubim" (<a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Book_of_Enoch/Chapter_20" class="extiw" title="s:Book of Enoch/Chapter 20">1 Enoch 20:7</a>).
</p><p>When Enoch asked who the four figures were that he had seen:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>And he said to me: 'This first is Michael, the merciful and long-suffering: and the second, who is set over all the diseases and all the wounds of the children of men, is Raphael: and the third, who is set over all the powers, is Gabriel: and the fourth, who is set over the repentance unto hope of those who inherit eternal life, is named Phanuel.' And these are the four angels of the Lord of Spirits and the four voices I heard in those days.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Book%20of%20Enoch/Chapter%2040">Enoch 40:9</a></cite></div></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gnosticism">Gnosticism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Gnosticism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heretical</a> Christian movement of <a href="/wiki/Gnosticism" title="Gnosticism">Gnosticism</a> paid special attention to angels as beings belonging to a pantheon of spiritual forces involved in the creation of the world. According to one ancient <a href="/wiki/Gnostic_texts" class="mw-redirect" title="Gnostic texts">Gnostic manuscript</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Book_of_the_Great_Invisible_Spirit" title="Holy Book of the Great Invisible Spirit">Holy Book of the Great Invisible Spirit</a>, Gabriel is a divine being and inhabitant of the <a href="/wiki/Pleroma#Gnosticism" title="Pleroma">Pleroma</a> who existed prior to the <a href="/wiki/Demiurge#Gnosticism" title="Demiurge">Demiurge</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Medieval_Christian_traditions">Medieval Christian traditions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Medieval Christian traditions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 155px">

</p>
			</div>
		</li>
</ul>
<p>In a famous early work, the "four homilies on the <i>Missus Est</i>", <a href="/wiki/Saint_Bernard_of_Clairvaux" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Bernard of Clairvaux">Saint Bernard of Clairvaux</a> (1090-1153 AD) interpreted Gabriel's name as "the strength of God", and his symbolic function in the gospel story as announcement of the strength or virtue of Christ, both as the strength of God incarnate and as the strength given by God to the timorous people who would bring into the world a fearful and troublesome event. "Therefore it was an opportune choice that designated Gabriel for the work he had to accomplish, or rather, because he was to accomplish it therefore he was called Gabriel."<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Feast_day">Feast day</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Feast day">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The feast day of Saint Gabriel the Archangel was exclusively celebrated on 18 March according to many sources dating between 1588 and 1921; unusually, a source published in 1856<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> has the feast celebrated on 7 April for unknown reasons (a parenthetical note states that the day is normally celebrated on 18 March). Writer <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Drayson" title="Elizabeth Drayson">Elizabeth Drayson</a> mentions the feast being celebrated on 18 March 1588 in her 2013 book "The Lead Books of Granada".<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of the oldest out-of-print sources placing the feast on 18 March, first published in 1608, is "Flos sanctorum: historia general de la vida y hechos de Jesu-Christo...y de los santos de que reza y haze fiesta la Iglesia Catholica..." by the <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spanish</a> writer <a href="/wiki/Alonso_de_Villegas" title="Alonso de Villegas">Alonso de Villegas</a>; a newer edition of this book was published in 1794.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Another source published in Ireland in 1886 the <i><a href="/wiki/Irish_Ecclesiastical_Record" title="Irish Ecclesiastical Record">Irish Ecclesiastical Record</a></i> also mentions 18 March.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The feast of Saint Gabriel was included by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XV" title="Pope Benedict XV">Pope Benedict XV</a> in the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a> in 1921, for celebration on 24 March.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> In 1969, the day was officially transferred to 29 September for celebration in conjunction with the feast of the archangels <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">St. Michael</a> and <a href="/wiki/Raphael_(archangel)" title="Raphael (archangel)">St. Raphael</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> has also adopted the 29 September date, known as <a href="/wiki/Michaelmas" title="Michaelmas">Michaelmas</a>.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> and those <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_Churches" title="Eastern Catholic Churches">Eastern Catholic Churches</a> that follow the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine Rite</a> celebrate his <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> (<a href="/w/index.php?title=Synaxis_of_the_Archangel_Michael_and_the_Other_Bodiless_Powers&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Synaxis of the Archangel Michael and the Other Bodiless Powers (page does not exist)">Synaxis of the Archangel Michael and the Other Bodiless Powers</a>) on 8 November (for those churches that follow the traditional <a href="/wiki/Julian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Julian Calendar">Julian Calendar</a>, 8 November currently falls on 21 November of the modern <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregorian Calendar">Gregorian Calendar</a>, a difference of 13 days). Eastern Orthodox commemorate him, not only on his November feast, but also on two other days:
</p>
<ul><li>26 March is the "<a href="/wiki/Synaxis" title="Synaxis">Synaxis</a> of the Archangel Gabriel" and celebrates his role in the <a href="/wiki/Annunciation" title="Annunciation">Annunciation</a> (eavetaking of the Annunciation)</li></ul>
<ul><li>13 July is also known as the "Synaxis of the Archangel Gabriel" and celebrates all the appearances and miracles attributed to Gabriel throughout history. The feast was first established on <a href="/wiki/Mount_Athos" title="Mount Athos">Mount Athos</a> when, in the 9th century, during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine Emperor">Emperor</a> <a href="/wiki/Basil_II" title="Basil II">Basil II</a> and Empress Constantina Porphyrogenitus and while <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_II_Chrysoberges" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicholas II Chrysoberges">Nicholas Chrysoverges</a> was <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople" title="Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople">Patriarch of Constantinople</a>, the Archangel appeared in a cell<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> near <a href="/wiki/Karyes" title="Karyes">Karyes</a>, where he wrote with his finger on a stone tablet the hymn to the <a href="/wiki/Theotokos" title="Theotokos">Theotokos</a>, "<a href="/wiki/Axion_Estin" title="Axion Estin">It is truly meet...</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>Saint Gabriel the Archangel is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">commemorated</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Vigil_(liturgy)" title="Vigil (liturgy)">vigil</a> of <a href="/wiki/Annunciation" title="Annunciation">Annunciation</a> (24 March) by <a href="/wiki/Antiochian_Western_Rite_Vicariate" title="Antiochian Western Rite Vicariate">Antiochian Western Rite Vicariate</a><sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/ROCOR" class="mw-redirect" title="ROCOR">ROCOR</a> <a href="/wiki/Western_Rite_Orthodoxy" title="Western Rite Orthodoxy">Western Rite</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church" title="Coptic Orthodox Church">Coptic Orthodox Church</a> celebrates his feast on 13 <a href="/wiki/Paoni" title="Paoni">Paoni</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> 22 <a href="/wiki/Koiak" title="Koiak">Koiak</a> and 26 <a href="/wiki/Paoni" title="Paoni">Paoni</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethiopian Church">Ethiopian Church</a> celebrates his feast on 18 (in the Ethiopian calendar) December, with a sizeable number of its believers making a pilgrimage to a church dedicated to "Saint Gabriel" in <a href="/wiki/Kulubi" title="Kulubi">Kulubi</a> and Wonkshet on that day.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran Church">Lutheran Churches</a>, Gabriel is celebrated on the Feast of the Archangels on 29 September.<sup id="cite_ref-Blersch2019_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Blersch2019-7">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Additionally, Gabriel is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of messengers, those who work for broadcasting and telecommunications such as radio and television, postal workers, clerics, diplomats, and stamp collectors.<sup id="cite_ref-Guiley2004p140_4-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guiley2004p140-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<dl><dt>Gabriel's horn</dt></dl>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Gabriel%27s_Horn" class="mw-redirect" title="Gabriel&#39;s Horn">Gabriel's Horn (Geometric figure)</a></div>
<p>A familiar <a href="/wiki/Literary_trope" class="mw-redirect" title="Literary trope">image</a> of Gabriel has him blowing a trumpet blast to announce the resurrection of the dead at the end of time. However, though the Bible mentions a trumpet blast preceding the resurrection of the dead, it never specifies Gabriel as the trumpeter. Different passages state different things: the angels of the Son of Man (<a href="/wiki/Matthew_24" title="Matthew 24">Matthew 24</a>:31); the voice of the Son of God (<a href="/wiki/John_5" title="John 5">John 5</a>:25-29); God's trumpet (<a href="/wiki/First_Epistle_to_the_Thessalonians" title="First Epistle to the Thessalonians">I Thessalonians</a> 4:16); seven angels sounding a series of blasts (<a href="/wiki/Revelation_8" title="Revelation 8">Revelation 8</a>-<a href="/wiki/Revelation_11" title="Revelation 11">11</a>); or simply "a trumpet will sound" (<a href="/wiki/First_Epistle_to_the_Corinthians" title="First Epistle to the Corinthians">I Corinthians</a> 15:52).<sup id="cite_ref-svm_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-svm-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Likewise the early Christian <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Fathers</a> do not mention Gabriel as a trumpeter; and in Jewish and Muslim traditions, Gabriel is again not identified as a trumpeter.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The earliest known identification of Gabriel as a trumpeter comes from the Hymn of the Armenian Saint Nerses Shnorhali, "for Protection in the Night":<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>The sound of Gabriel's trumpet on the last night, make us worthy to hear, and to stand on your right hand among the sheep with lanterns of inextinguishable light; to be like the five wise virgins, so that with the bridegroom in the bride chamber we, his spiritual brides may enter into glory.</p></blockquote>
<p>In 1455, in <a href="/wiki/Armenian_art" title="Armenian art">Armenian art</a>, there is an illustration in an Armenian manuscript showing Gabriel sounding his trumpet as the dead climb out of their graves.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Evangelical_Christian_traditions">Evangelical Christian traditions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Evangelical Christian traditions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The image of Gabriel's trumpet blast to announce the end of time became was taken up in <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Evangelical Christianity">Evangelical Christianity</a>, where it became widespread, notably in <a href="/wiki/Negro_spirituals" class="mw-redirect" title="Negro spirituals">Negro spirituals</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
An early example occurs in <a href="/wiki/John_Milton" title="John Milton">John Milton</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Paradise_Lost" title="Paradise Lost">Paradise Lost</a></i> (1667):<sup id="cite_ref-svm_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-svm-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><div class="poem">
<p>Betwixt these rockie pillars Gabriel sat<br />
Chief of the Angelic guards (IV.545f)...<br />
He ended, and the Son gave signal high<br />
To the bright minister that watch'd, he blew<br />
His trumpet, heard in Oreb since perhaps<br />
When God descended, and perhaps once more<br />
To sound at general doom. (XI.72ff).
</p>
</div></blockquote>
<p>It is unclear how the Armenian conception inspired Milton and the spirituals, though they presumably have a common source.<sup id="cite_ref-svm_37-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-svm-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Latter-day_Saints">Latter-day Saints</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Latter-day Saints">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" title="The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</a> theology, Gabriel is believed to have lived a mortal life as the prophet <a href="/wiki/Noah" title="Noah">Noah</a>. The two are regarded as the same individual; Noah being his mortal name and Gabriel being his heavenly name.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:2_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Islam"><span class="anchor" id="Gabriel_in_Islam"></span><span class="anchor" id="Jibril"></span>Islam</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Islam">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/R%C5%AB%E1%B8%A5" title="Rūḥ">Rūḥ</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Gibril" and "Jibril" redirect here. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Gibril_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Gibril (disambiguation)">Gibril (disambiguation)</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jibril_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Jibril (disambiguation)">Jibril (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<p>Gabriel (<a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>: <span lang="ar" dir="rtl">جِبْرِيل</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic" title="Romanization of Arabic">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Arabic-language romanization"><i lang="ar-Latn">Jibrīl</i></span>; also <a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>: <span lang="ar" dir="rtl">جبرائيل</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic" title="Romanization of Arabic">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Arabic-language romanization"><i lang="ar-Latn">Jibrāʾīl</i></span> or <i>Jabrāʾīl</i>, derived from the <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>: <span lang="he" dir="rtl">גַּבְרִיאֵל</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Hebrew" title="Romanization of Hebrew">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Hebrew-language romanization"><i lang="he-Latn">Gaḇrīʾēl</i></span>)<sup id="cite_ref-EoQ_9-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EoQ-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Reynolds_2014_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reynolds_2014-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-EI2_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EI2-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> is venerated as one of the primary <a href="/wiki/Archangel" title="Archangel">archangels</a> and as the Angel of Revelation in <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-EoQ_9-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EoQ-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Reynolds_2014_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reynolds_2014-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-EI2_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EI2-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> He is primarily mentioned in the verses <span class="plainlinks"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://quran.com/2?startingVerse=97">2:97</a></span>, <span class="plainlinks"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://quran.com/2?startingVerse=98">2:98</a></span> and <span class="plainlinks"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://quran.com/66?startingVerse=4">66:4</a></span> of the <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a>, although the Quranic text doesn't explicitly refer to him as an angel.<sup id="cite_ref-Reynolds_2014_45-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reynolds_2014-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> In the Quran, the archangel Gabriel appears named in <span class="plainlinks"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://quran.com/2?startingVerse=97">2:97</a></span> and <span class="plainlinks"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://quran.com/66?startingVerse=4">66:4</a></span>, as well as in <span class="plainlinks"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://quran.com/2?startingVerse=98">2:98</a></span>, where he is mentioned along with the archangel <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Michael</a> (<a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)#Islam" title="Michael (archangel)">Mīkāʾīl</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-EoQ_9-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EoQ-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Tafsir" title="Tafsir">Exegetical Quranic literature</a> narrates that <a href="/wiki/Muhammad" title="Muhammad">Muhammad</a> saw the archangel Gabriel in his full angelic splendor only twice, the first time being when he received his first revelation.<sup id="cite_ref-EI2_46-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EI2-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> As the Bible portrays Gabriel as a celestial messenger sent to <a href="/wiki/Daniel_(biblical_figure)" title="Daniel (biblical figure)">Daniel</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Mary_(mother_of_Jesus)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary (mother of Jesus)">Mary</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Zechariah_(priest)" class="mw-redirect" title="Zechariah (priest)">Zechariah</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Islamic tradition holds that Gabriel was sent to numerous pre-Islamic Biblical prophets with revelation and divine injunctions, including <a href="/wiki/Adam#In_Islam" title="Adam">Adam</a>, whom Muslims believe was consoled by Gabriel some time after <a href="/wiki/Fall_of_man" title="Fall of man">the Fall</a>, too.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> He is known by many names in Islam, such as "keeper of holiness".<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> In Hadith traditions, Jibril is said to have six hundred wings.<sup id="cite_ref-Sunnah.com_Muslim_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sunnah.com_Muslim-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tasks">Tasks</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Tasks">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Muslims believe that Gabriel was mainly tasked with transmitting the scriptures from God to the <a href="/wiki/Prophets_and_messengers_in_Islam" title="Prophets and messengers in Islam">prophets and messengers</a>, as <a href="/wiki/Asbab_al-Nuzul" title="Asbab al-Nuzul">Asbab al-Nuzul</a> or revelation<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> when Muhammad was questioned which angel is revealing the holy scriptures revelation, and Muhammad told the Jews it is revealed by Gabriel who is tasked to it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoegelWheeler2002218_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoegelWheeler2002218-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Muslims also revere Gabriel for a number of events predating what they regard as the first revelation, narrated in the Quran. Muslims believe that Gabriel was the angel who informed Zachariah (<a href="/wiki/Zechariah_in_Islam" title="Zechariah in Islam">Zakariyyā</a>) of     Yaḥyā's (<a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist" title="John the Baptist">John's</a>) <a href="/wiki/Nativity_of_St_John_the_Baptist" class="mw-redirect" title="Nativity of St John the Baptist">birth</a>, as well as Mary (<a href="/wiki/Islamic_views_of_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Islamic views of Mary">Maryam</a>) of the future <a href="/wiki/Nativity_of_Jesus" title="Nativity of Jesus">nativity of Jesus</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> and that Gabriel was one of three angels who had earlier informed <a href="/wiki/Abraham" title="Abraham">Abraham</a> (ʾIbrāhīm) of the birth of <a href="/wiki/Isaac_in_Islam" title="Isaac in Islam">Isaac</a> (<span class="plainlinks"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://quran.com/51?startingVerse=24">51:24-30</a></span>).<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Gabriel also makes a famous appearance in the <a href="/wiki/Hadith_of_Gabriel" title="Hadith of Gabriel">Hadith of Gabriel</a>, in which he questions Muhammad on the core tenets of Islam.<sup id="cite_ref-EoQ_9-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EoQ-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gabriel is also believed to have delivered punishment from God to the <a href="/wiki/Sodomites_in_Islam" class="mw-redirect" title="Sodomites in Islam">Sodomite</a> by leveling the entire Sodom city with a tip of his wing.<sup id="cite_ref-Nabi-Nabi_Allah:_Kisah_Para_Nabi_dan_Rasul_Allah_dalam_Al-Qur&#39;an_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nabi-Nabi_Allah:_Kisah_Para_Nabi_dan_Rasul_Allah_dalam_Al-Qur&#39;an-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> According to a Hadith narrated by <a href="/wiki/Abu_Dharr_al-Ghifari" title="Abu Dharr al-Ghifari">Abu Dharr al-Ghifari</a>, which is compiled by <a href="/wiki/Al-Hakim_al-Tirmidhi" title="Al-Hakim al-Tirmidhi">al-Hakim al-Tirmidhi</a>, Gabriel has an ability to regulate <a href="/wiki/Feeling" title="Feeling">Feeling</a> or <a href="/wiki/Perception" title="Perception">Perception</a> of human, particularly a feel of happiness or sadness.<sup id="cite_ref-Suyuti;_angel_Jibril_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Suyuti;_angel_Jibril-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Gabriel is believed to have helped Muhammad overcome his adversaries significantly against a <a href="/wiki/Ifrit" title="Ifrit">demon</a> (<i>ʻifrīt</i>) during the <a href="/wiki/Isra_and_Mi%27raj" class="mw-redirect" title="Isra and Mi&#39;raj">Mi'raj</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Gabriel is believed to have helped Muhammad overcome his adversaries during the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Badr" title="Battle of Badr">Battle of Badr</a>, where according to scholars and clerics of Islam, the various hadiths, both authentics and inauthentics, has mentioned that Gabriel,<sup id="cite_ref-Misri;_Sirah_Sahabah:_Zubair_bin_Awwam_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Misri;_Sirah_Sahabah:_Zubair_bin_Awwam-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Michael</a>, <a href="/wiki/Israfil" title="Israfil">Raphael</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Israfil_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Israfil-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;N 3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;N 4&#93;</a></sup> and thousands of <a href="/wiki/Angels_in_Islam" title="Angels in Islam">best angels</a> from third level of heaven, all came to the battle of Badr by impersonating appearance of <a href="/wiki/Zubayr_ibn_al-Awwam" title="Zubayr ibn al-Awwam">Zubayr ibn al-Awwam</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Companions_of_the_Prophet" title="Companions of the Prophet">Companions of the Prophet</a> and bodyguard of the prophet.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;N 5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Tarikh_Dimashq_2_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tarikh_Dimashq_2-72">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> This is deemed as Zubayr personal honor according to Islamic belief.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERizqullah2005410_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERizqullah2005410-73">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAbasoomarAbasoomar2016_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbasoomarAbasoomar2016-74">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;N 6&#93;</a></sup> Meanwhile, <a href="/wiki/Safiur_Rahman_Mubarakpuri" title="Safiur Rahman Mubarakpuri">Safiur Rahman Mubarakpuri</a> has recorded in his <a href="/wiki/Historiography" title="Historiography">historiography</a> works of Quran and Hadith revelation in <a href="/wiki/Prophetic_biography" title="Prophetic biography">Prophetic biography</a>, that <a href="/wiki/Sa%27d_ibn_Abi_Waqqas" title="Sa&#39;d ibn Abi Waqqas">Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas</a> testified he saw two unidentified warriors clad in white has protected Muhammad during the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Uhud" title="Battle of Uhud">Battle of Uhud</a>, That later being confirmed by Muhammad those two unidentified warriors were Jibril and <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Mikail</a> in disguise.<sup id="cite_ref-eve_of_battle_of_Uhud_&amp;_Ahzab_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-eve_of_battle_of_Uhud_&amp;_Ahzab-77">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Moreover, he is believed to have further encouraged Muhammad to wage war and attack the <a href="/wiki/Jewish_tribes_of_Arabia" title="Jewish tribes of Arabia">Jewish tribe</a> of <a href="/wiki/Banu_Qurayza" title="Banu Qurayza">Banu Qurayza</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Reynolds_2014_45-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reynolds_2014-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ṣaḥīḥ_Al-Bukhārī_The_Translation_of_the_Meanings_of_Sahih_Al-Bukhari_:_Arabic-English_·_Volume_5_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ṣaḥīḥ_Al-Bukhārī_The_Translation_of_the_Meanings_of_Sahih_Al-Bukhari_:_Arabic-English_·_Volume_5-78">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Another appearance of Gabriel in Islamic religious texts were found in numerous Hadiths during the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Hunayn" title="Battle of Hunayn">Battle of Hunayn</a>, where the Gabriel stood next to Muhammad.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Book_of_the_Jihad_of_&#39;Ali_Ibn_Tahir_Al-Sulami_(d._1106)_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Book_of_the_Jihad_of_&#39;Ali_Ibn_Tahir_Al-Sulami_(d._1106)-79">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Other <a href="/wiki/Islamic_holy_books" title="Islamic holy books">Islamic texts</a> and some <a href="/wiki/Apocryphal_literature" class="mw-redirect" title="Apocryphal literature">Apocryphal literature</a> also supported Gabriel's role as a celestial warrior.<sup id="cite_ref-Reynolds_2014_45-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reynolds_2014-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Though alternate theories exist, whether the occurrence of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Spirit" title="Holy Spirit">Holy Spirit</a> in the Quran refers to Gabriel or not, remains an issue of scholarly debate.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> However, a clear distinction between apocryphal and Quranic references to Gabriel is that the former doesn't designate him as the Holy Spirit in the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Enoch" title="Book of Enoch">First Book of Enoch</a>, which narrates the story of Gabriel defeating the <a href="/wiki/Nephilim" title="Nephilim">Nephilim</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Reynolds_2014_45-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reynolds_2014-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Yezidi_tradition">Yezidi tradition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Yezidi tradition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><a href="/wiki/Yazidis#Religion" title="Yazidis">Yazidis</a> consider Gabriel one of the <a href="/wiki/Yazidism" title="Yazidism">Seven Mysteries</a>, the heptad to which God entrusted the world, and sometimes identified with the archangel <a href="/wiki/Melek_Taus" class="mw-redirect" title="Melek Taus">Melek Taus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Art.2C_entertainment.2C_and_media"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Art,_entertainment,_and_media">Art, entertainment, and media</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Art, entertainment, and media">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Angels are described as pure spirits.<sup id="cite_ref-gorgievski_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gorgievski-82">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-longhurst_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-longhurst-83">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> The lack of a defined form allows artists wide latitude in depicting them.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Amelia R. Brown draws comparisons in Byzantine iconography between portrayals of angels and the conventions used to depict court eunuchs. Mainly from the Caucasus, they tended to have light eyes, hair, and skin; and those "castrated in childhood developed a distinctive skeletal structure, lacked full masculine musculature, body hair and beards...." As officials, they would wear a white tunic decorated with gold. Brown suggests that  "Byzantine artists drew, consciously or not, on this iconography of the court eunuch."<sup id="cite_ref-brown_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brown-85">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Some recent popular works on angels consider Gabriel to be <a href="/wiki/Female" title="Female">female</a> or <a href="/wiki/Androgynous" class="mw-redirect" title="Androgynous">androgynous</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Giovetti1993_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Giovetti1993-86">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Godwin1990_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Godwin1990-87">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gabriel_sculptures">Gabriel sculptures</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gabriel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Gabriel sculptures">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
